Koslowsky, Kieran Lehan, Lindsey Langs, James MacDonald, Michael
Solohub, Jacob Staines, Greg Galloway, Sean Carey, Rosy Tutton, David
Barrett, and Tyler De Jong for their help with data collection and Tom
Brown and Logan Fang for support of the CRHM platform.

\section{Data and Software Availability
Statement}\label{data-and-software-availability-statement}

The Cold Regions Hydrological Model Platform (CRHM) source code used in
this study is preserved at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.17981431
(Pomeroy et al., 2025), available under the GPL-3.0 licence, and
developed openly at https://github.com/srlabUsask/crhmcode. Model
forcing data, model outputs, validation data, processed data, and
scripts to run the processing are available at
https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.17409551 with GPL-3.0 licence access
conditions.

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Synthesis and Conclusion}\label{synthesis-and-conclusion}

Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) Transparency Statement: GenAI
tools (i.e., ChatGPT, Grammarly) were used sparingly and responsibly in
this chapter to enhance text clarity.

\section{Closing the Research Gap}\label{closing-the-research-gap}

Needleleaf forest canopies can intercept a large fraction of snowfall
and alter the phase, timing, and quantity of precipitation reaching the
ground. In cold continental climates, up to half of annual snowfall may
be lost to the atmosphere via sublimation of intercepted snow, and in
warmer climates, higher melt rates cause a large fraction of snowfall to
reach the ground as liquid water. Sparse observations of snow
interception, canopy snow ablation, and forest snow
accumulation---primarily located in cold continental climates or warm
maritime climates with dense forest canopies---have led to process
representations that have uncertain extrapolation across the globe.
Existing theories and resulting parameterisations commonly used in
hydrological and land-surface models to represent snow interception and
canopy snow ablation include: observations from cold-continental boreal
forests for interception and unloading (Ellis et al., 2010; Hedstrom \&
Pomeroy, 1998) and sublimation of intercepted snow (Pomeroy et al.,
1998b); observations from warm maritime climates for interception,
unloading, and drip (Andreadis et al., 2009; Storck et al., 2002); and
an unloading model based on indirect measurements of intercepted snow
ablation at several sites (Roesch et al., 2001). Snow interception and
canopy snow ablation processes exhibit a strong dependence on both
meteorology and canopy density, leading to differing process emergence
in theories developed in these distinctive climates, seasons, and forest
types and limited transferability to new regions. Differing
methodologies used to develop existing theories have also introduced
uncertainty regarding the extent to which snow interception and canopy
snow ablation processes are captured, leading to further differences in
processes represented by current models. As a result, existing models
exhibit high variability and uncertainty in predicting land-surface
albedo, snow accumulation, melt, and subsequent runoff or infiltration
in forested snow-dominated basin. Moreover, as the global climate warms
there is a need to ensure existing theories to represent snow
interception processes are robust across a range of meteorological
conditions and evolving forests. The aim of this research was to
evaluate the suitability of existing theories of snow interception and
canopy snow ablation on their transferability across a wide range of
climate and forest types. This thesis also links this improved process
understanding to model development and validation to test new theories
across a range of climate and forest types.

In Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-2}, a comprehensive literature review was
conducted to establish major gaps in the literature. In this review the
theories and assumptions underpinning existing snow interception and
canopy snow ablation parameterisations are presented. Through this
review it was revealed that there has been limited research on the
transferability of existing parameterisations across a range of canopy
densities or meteorological conditions. Moreover, existing discussions
of differing process behaviour and emergence in existing models is
typically equated to differing environmental conditions in previous
studies. However, this work outlines that differing methodological
approaches for measuring individual processes also contributes to the
differing relationships that have been developed. Existing theories of
initial interception (Hedstrom \& Pomeroy, 1998; Satterlund \& Haupt,
1967; Schmidt \& Gluns, 1991), represent a strong increase in
throughfall with increasing canopy snow load and air temperature.
However, the long duration of snowfall events (Hedstrom \& Pomeroy,
1998; Schmidt \& Gluns, 1991) and temperatures occasionally near the
melting point (Satterlund \& Haupt, 1967) greatly increased the
probability of unloading within these measurements. This mixing of
processes in measurements and resulting parameterisations introduces
potential double counting of processes in hydrological models if these
initial interception parameterisations were also combined with
comprehensive intercepted snow unloading routines. It was also
identified that previous snow interception parameterisations were
developed on small spatial scales such as point (Storck et al., 2002) or
transect (Hedstrom \& Pomeroy, 1998) in distinctive climates, thus
adding uncertainty to their transferability across diverse canopy
structures over larger scales. Therefore, it was determined that
additional observations of throughfall and snow interception are needed
that minimise the inclusion of ablation processes, and span a gradient
of meteorological conditions and canopy densities. In addition to these
gaps regarding the initial interception process, canopy snow unloading
measurements are reported by previous studies (Floyd, 2012; Storck et
al., 2002) to be difficult to collect due to their simultaneous
occurrence with other ablation processes and has led to limited and
sparse measurements. As a result, existing dry-snow unloading
parameterisations are based on indirect measurements of canopy snow
unloading such as changes in above canopy albedo (Bartlett \& Verseghy,
2015; Roesch et al., 2001) which may also incorporate other ablation
processes than unloading (i.e., sublimation losses). Moreover,
differentiating between ablation of intercepted snow as solid
(unloading) and liquid (drip) during unloading is also reported as a
challenge in the literature due to freeze-thaw processes that impact
instrument functionality. Thus, resulting parameterisations to represent
the partitioning of intercepted snow into liquid meltwater and solid
snow unloading are based on limited observations from limited events
from two sites (Floyd, 2012; Storck et al., 2002). To address this
research gap, additional direct measurements of canopy snow unloading
and drip were identified as necessary, alongside hybrid approaches to
differentiate processes when they cannot be measured. An overarching
theme from this review is the need to rigorously evaluate the theories
underlying existing parameterisations using observations that isolate
individual processes within the canopy snow mass and energy balance.

Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-3} aimed to address several research gaps
regarding understanding the initial interception of snow in needleleaf
canopies across a broad range in canopy density and meteorologies. The
study assessed relationships between initial interception, canopy snow
load, meteorology, and canopy density using observations that limited
the effect of intercepted snow ablation processes. The first research
gap this study addressed was that established theories regarding maximum
canopy snow load capacity and the influence of air temperature on
initial interception (Andreadis et al., 2009; Ellis et al., 2010;
Hedstrom \& Pomeroy, 1998; Storck et al., 2002) were not observed at
this study site. This discrepancy is likely because earlier studies
incorporated unloading processes within their interception measurements.
It was hypothesised that branch compression, influenced by both air
temperature and canopy snow load, together with the expansion of contact
area as snow bridged canopy gaps and variations in snow adhesion and
cohesion, offset these opposing relationships with interception, thereby
limiting their detection in this study. A second research gap this
chapter addressed was to investigate the relationship of initial snow
interception across a broad range in canopy densities. In a subalpine
forest with variable canopy density, a strong linear relationship was
found between initial interception efficiency and the snow-leaf contact
area, a metric that is a function of canopy coverage and hydrometeor
trajectory angle---estimated as a function of wind speed and hydrometeor
velocity. An existing relationship (Hedstrom \& Pomeroy, 1998) to
describe the change in leaf contact area with hydrometeor trajectory
angle did not match the observations collected in this study. The
invalidation of existing interception theory assumptions---particularly
regarding maximum canopy snow load and air temperature---combined with
newly identified relationships between interception and leaf contact
area, provided motivation to development two improved parameterisations
for initial interception. The first parameterisation approximates
snow-leaf contact area using wind speed, hydrometeor velocity, and nadir
canopy cover. The second parameterisation, calculates initial
interception as a function of leaf contact area, similar to rainfall
interception models which compute initial interception as a function of
canopy coverage and separate initial rainfall interception, evaporation,
and drip processes. However, it differs from existing rainfall
approaches by accounting for changes in snow--leaf contact area
associated with the snowfall trajectory angle, which is necessary given
the lower terminal fall velocity of snowflakes compared to raindrops.
This approach improved the prediction of initial interception efficiency
and throughfall across a broad range in canopy densities and
meteorological conditions. By minimising ablation in the initial
interception measurements, this model is expected to be more compatible
with intercepted snow ablation parameterisations that include a
comprehensive representation of unloading, melt/drip, and sublimation
processes.

In Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-4}, new observations of canopy intercepted
snow ablation were presented---collected over events with meteorological
conditions representing climates ranging from the cold-continental to
temperate-maritime---and provided new insights on the processes
governing unloading and melt/drip of intercepted snow. The direct
measurements of canopy snow unloading presented here provide an
important contribution to the literature as previous studies typically
relied on albedo observations to infer snow unloading (Bartlett \&
Verseghy, 2015; Roesch et al., 2001) to develop parameterisations and
may have been influenced by other processes than unloading (e.g., above
canopy albedo is influenced by unloading, melt, and sublimation).
Moreover, observations of melt-induced unloading were previously limited
to two sites (Floyd, 2012; Storck et al., 2002) with limited variability
in canopy snow loads. These new observations identified canopy snow
load, wind shear stress, and the canopy snowmelt rate as primary
predictors of unloading. The findings align with previous studies
emphasizing the importance of representing both cold/dry snow unloading
and snowmelt-induced unloading processes. Although canopy snow load,
wind, and melt have been widely recognised as important controls on
unloading, many existing modelling approaches struggle to reproduce
unloading behaviour across both cold, dry conditions and melt periods.
These deficiencies largely reflect either the absence of an explicit
wind-driven unloading mechanism or the treatment of intercepted snowmelt
and associated unloading using simplified temperature-based
formulations. The relationships observed here incorporated predictors
with stronger physical foundations. For example, using wind shear stress
provided improved predictive power than wind speed, likely due to its
more direct representation of the mechanical forces acting on
intercepted snow. Similarly, a physically based energy balance approach
to melt and unload intercepted snow offered improved performance
compared to temperature index models (Ellis et al., 2010; Roesch et al.,
2001). In addition, a new linear relationship was identified, showing
that the amount of solid snow unloading during melt events increased
proportionally with canopy snow load. Collectively, these findings
underscore the importance of representing both dry and melt-induced
unloading and prioritizing physically based methods. They also provide
new context for the maximum canopy snow load used in previous
interception and ablation models, demonstrating that unloading increases
proportionally with canopy snow load rather than after reaching a fixed
threshold. Moreover, the weak influence of canopy snow load on
throughfall and canopy coverage observed in Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-3}
may be explained as unloading occurs before canopy coverage can be
substantially modified by intercepted snow load.

In Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-5}, new parameterisations to represent the
mass and energy balance of snow intercepted in the canopy---developed in
Chapters 3 and 4---were incorporated as a new module in CRHM. An
evaluation of the quantity and duration of SWE intercepted in the canopy
was found to improve at one new site not used in model development, in
addition to the evaluation from a second site in
Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-4}. The updated model also resulted in
substantial reduction in errors in simulating subcanopy SWE at the
temperate Russell Creek site due to the better physical representation
of the intercepted snowmelt, drip, and unloading processes compared to
the existing CRHM model. The reduction in subcanopy SWE error by the new
model was smaller at the three cold climate sites (Fortress, Marmot, and
Wolf Creek), however, there is a large difference in the amount and
duration of snow load intercepted in the canopy across all sites.
Although the net quantity of snow/meltwater reaching the ground was
relatively similar in the three colder climate sites between the two
models the large difference in simulated canopy snow load has
implications for surface albedo representation in land-surface schemes.
The results presented here suggest improved transferability of the new
model in representing the canopy snow mass and energy balance across
diverse canopy structures and climatic conditions. These results also
highlight the strong dependence of forest-snow interactions on both
climate and canopy structure resulting in a large variability in the
quantity and phase of snowfall reaching the ground across all sites. At
two lower snowfall sites with calm winds and cold temperatures, roughly
half of annual snowfall was lost to the atmosphere via sublimation of
intercepted snow due to lower canopy snow loads and wind-induced
unloading resulting in reduced canopy snow unloading rates. A site with
higher wind speed and cold temperatures, resulted in higher unloading
rates which in turn reduced sublimation of intercepted snow. Russell
Creek had nearly half of its seasonal snowfall melt in the canopy due to
warm temperatures and high humidity. This high melt rate, coupled with
increased melt-induced unloading rates and reduced vapour pressure
deficits, led to very low sublimation losses. Across the four sites,
climate and canopy density were found to be more important drivers for
influencing snow interception and subsequent canopy snow ablation
processes than species-level differences among fir- and spruce-dominated
forests.

\section{How Does This Research Advance
Science?}\label{how-does-this-research-advance-science}

\subsection{Process-based Diagnosis of
Theory}\label{process-based-diagnosis-of-theory}

Many advances in hydrology have arisen from empirically diagnosing
individual processes to improve their representation in hydrological
models (Gray et al., 1985; Groff \& Pomeroy, 2025; Ireson et al., 2022;
Pomeroy et al., 1993; Pradhananga \& Pomeroy, 2022). The robustness of
such parameterisations depends on the quality of the underlying
observations, making detailed measurements essential. This thesis placed
particular emphasis on constructing a methodological framework focused
on the careful collection of observations to enable a better
process-based analysis of theory, compared to previous approaches which
often combined initial snow interception with subsequent canopy snow
ablation processes. As a result, the framework used in this thesis
allowed throughfall and interception measurements to be better isolated
from canopy snow ablation processes contributing to an assessment of
snow interception relationships that better reflects the individual
process. These process measurements were also collected across varying
canopy densities and meteorological conditions providing an evaluation
of relationships across diverse conditions compared to existing studies
which focused only on dense forest canopies in cold-continental
(Hedstrom \& Pomeroy, 1998) and warm-maritime (Storck et al., 2002)
climates. New advances in UAV-borne lidar technology allow the
collection of canopy density and structure measurements over large areas
(Moeser et al., 2015a; Roth \& Nolin, 2019; Staines \& Pomeroy, 2023),
however corresponding process measurements at matching high resolution
(i.e., throughfall) have been limited. This thesis provides the first
linkage between high resolution throughfall observations and canopy
structure derived from aerial lidar while also integrating subcanopy
lysimeter measurements of throughfall and unloading at a high temporal
frequency, allowing for the evaluation of theory across a broad range in
canopy densities and meteorologies.

\subsection{Advancing Theories of Snowfall Interception and Ablation in
Needleleaf Forest
Canopies}\label{advancing-theories-of-snowfall-interception-and-ablation-in-needleleaf-forest-canopies}

In addition to the evaluation of existing theories with field
measurements, this thesis developed a comprehensive framework that
connects new insights on snow interception processes to both process
understanding and practical steps for overcoming limitations of current
approaches in hydrological models. New parameterisations were introduced
to represent initial snow interception and canopy snow ablation
processes which demonstrated improved accuracy in representing canopy
snow load and subcanopy snow accumulation across diverse forest and
climate types. By more explicitly representing the underlying physical
processes, these parameterisations reduce the possibility of
approximating the right answer for the wrong reason and enhance the
potential for transferability to new sites.

The new relationships revealed through this work highlight the
variability of snow interception processes in sparse forest canopies
across varying canopy densities. It was shown this variability could be
represented using commonly available input data (wind speed, canopy
cover, and hydrometeor fall velocity) across a wide range of
meteorological conditions. However, most hydrological and land-surface
models classify land cover into broad forested and non-forested
categories, failing to capture the heterogeneity of snow processes in
these environments. Results from this thesis demonstrate that
interception efficiency can vary by a factor of two in sparse canopies
with wind speeds from 0 to 1 m s\textsuperscript{-1}---variability that
existing theories cannot resolve. This finding highlights the importance
of treating forests as a continuous distribution of canopy densities in
order to more accurately represent the variability of possible initial
interception efficiencies in these diverse forests. The new approach not
only advances representation of snow accumulation in forests but also
aligns more closely with the rainfall interception literature, providing
a more consistent and transferable framework for hydrological and
land-surface model development.

New relationships, building on new observations and existing theory,
were developed to represent canopy snow unloading and melt, resulting in
improved estimates of subcanopy SWE, canopy snow load, and individual
processes (sublimation, unloading, and melt/drip). These advances
highlight the importance of a comprehensive coupled representation of
the canopy snow mass and energy balance. Parameters for initial
interception and unloading are now more directly linked to the processes
they represent, improving the clarity and interpretability of model
calculations. Collectively, the new parameterisations better capture
individual forest-snow processes and demonstrate stronger
transferability across sites and under future climate conditions.

\subsection{Model Framework for Understanding Changes to Snow Storage in
Forested
Basins}\label{model-framework-for-understanding-changes-to-snow-storage-in-forested-basins}

These results provide new a diagnostic framework to investigate how
climate and vegetation change will alter snow interception and ablation
in needleleaf canopies, and have the potential to increase understanding
of implications on downstream water resources. Both observations and
simulations from this research demonstrate that snow interception
processes vary substantially with climatic regime and canopy density.
These findings also extend earlier research by Pomeroy \& Gray (1995)
who observed differences in interception loss across climates, by
revealing the proportion of processes governing the partitioning of
intercepted snow---including canopy snow unloading, sublimation, and
drip/melt---across temperate and cold-regions forests. The new model
showed large variations in the processes behind snow interception loss,
with sublimation of intercepted snow as the primary driver in
cold-regions forests and canopy snowmelt in temperate forests. The
improved transferability of parameterisations across diverse climatic
conditions and forest types strengthens confidence in using this new
model to diagnose future changes in forested basin snow storage under
climate warming and vegetation shifts. These findings also highlight the
risks of extrapolating snow storage estimates from open sites to
forested areas in an evolving environment. As temperatures rise, snow
interception regimes may transition from sublimation-dominated processes
to melt-dominated ones, altering both the timing and quantity of snow
reaching the ground thus changing relationships between snow storage in
open environments and total basin snow water storage. The
parameterisations and model framework developed in this thesis provide
tools to diagnose and predict such transitions, with direct implications
for snow hydrology in forested basins and for land-surface modelling
schemes due to the influence of intercepted snow on albedo.

\section{Limitations and Future
Directions}\label{limitations-and-future-directions}

Future work is needed to continue to improve understanding of snow
interception and ablation in needleleaf canopies, and to mobilise these
findings into decision-making tools for managing water resources in
snow-dominated forested basins. The empirical coefficients developed to
represent initial interception, canopy density, and unloading require
further validation across differing climatic conditions and forest
compositions of varying species and ages. While these coefficients could
be calibrated to reduce errors in subcanopy SWE or streamflow, this
risks model equifinality---producing correct outputs through incorrect
process representation (Beven, 2006). A more robust approach, as
demonstrated in this thesis, constrain parameters using process-level
observations of the corresponding process. This method could be extended
to determine appropriate parameter ranges using similar observations to
those collected in this thesis across various forest types, structures,
and climates.

This study focused on forests composed of needleleaf species (e.g.,
pine, spruce, and fir); consequently, the applicability of the
theoretical framework and associated empirical coefficients to forests
of different ages or to species with alternative leaf structures---such
as scale-leaved western red cedar (\emph{Thuja plicata}) or broadleaf
canopies, including structurally distinct canopies in the Southern
Hemisphere (e.g., Huerta et al., 2019)---remains untested. Although
cohesion and adhesion forces showed minimal variation in this study,
their known temperature dependence warrants investigation into whether
explicitly representing these processes improves interception efficiency
calculations in other environments (Kobayashi, 1987; Pfister \&
Schneebeli, 1999). Additionally, the species-specific canopy load
capacities used in previous models (Andreadis et al., 2009; Hedstrom \&
Pomeroy, 1998) likely relate to the unloading rate coefficients
presented here. These parameters may implicitly capture differences in
branch rigidity and size associated with species, age, and temperature,
which influence branch deformation under increasing snow load and, in
turn, affect unloading rates and their relationship with snow load.
However, additional observations of canopy snow unloading across
differing forest types are needed to confirm this. Moreover, rainfall
interception studies have demonstrated that liquid water storage
capacity varies with tree age class (e.g., Pypker et al., 2005b) and
species (e.g., Xiao \& McPherson, 2016), implying that canopy snow
unloading rates may also differ across age classes and tree species. The
canopy snow unloading measurements presented here were limited to four
instruments installed within a mixed fir and spruce forest plot and thus
limited interpretation on how varied species and forest
structure/density influences canopy snow unloading could be ascertained
from these datasets. Moreover, validation of the new model in simulating
canopy snow load relied on observations from weighed trees that were
mature (\textasciitilde10 m) fir and spruce. Consequently, the
transferability of these results to forests dominated by younger trees
or species with more flexible and less supportive branch structures
(e.g., cedar, hemlock) remains uncertain. Future research should collect
observations across a wider range of species, ages, and canopy densities
to better constrain estimates of interception and unloading
coefficients.

The new canopy snow model requires estimates of within-canopy wind flow
to calculate the initial interception and canopy snow energy balance
parameterisations, as well as within-canopy shear stress to simulate
wind-driven unloading of intercepted snow. While site-specific
observations enabled robust development of empirical within-canopy wind
flow relationships at Fortress (Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-3};
Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-4}); however, the reliance on these
relationships in Appendix~\ref{sec-appdx-ch5} added uncertainty when the
new model was applied at other sites. Future improvements could be
achieved through more physically based representations of within-canopy
wind flow that explicitly account for forest structural characteristics
and variations in air density (Stull, 2017).

A key process that is likely important to represent in transitional or
maritime climates is the accumulation of ice in the canopy through
freeze-thaw cycles, freezing rain, frost deposition, and rime accretion
(Berndt \& Fowler, 1969; Lumbrazo et al., 2022). These ice chunks can
engulf branches and resist unloading while also potentially altering
turbulent energy exchanges by reducing wind flow compared to more porous
snow clumps (Lumbrazo et al., 2022). A canopy ice reservoir could be
added to better represent the differing mass and energy balance compared
to intercepted snow. However, since this process was not observed in
this thesis, it could not be tested empirically and thus was not
implemented. Moreover, investigations of the liquid water holding
capacity of intercepted snow and vegetation elements could help the
representation of refreezing of liquid water intercepted in the canopy.

Collection of subcanopy SWE over large spatial extents with aerial lidar
derived subcanopy snow depths, and converted to SWE using observed or
simulated snow densities such as in Staines \& Pomeroy (2023) and
Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-3}, have the potential to validate updates to
the snow interception and canopy snow ablation parameterisations
presented here over large spatial extents which could help further build
confidence in these models. However, it will be important to consider
the uncertainty in meteorological forcing which becomes larger, further
away from station observations (e.g., Tang et al., 2023) which will
contribute to errors in simulated SWE in addition to the new
parameterisations that represent snowfall partitioning by forest
canopies. Moreover, accurately representing surface snowpack processes
such as rain-on-snow, melt, sublimation, and wind redistribution will
also play a role in achieving accurate simulations of observed subcanopy
SWE, in addition to the improved representation of the canopy snow mass
and energy balance.

The widespread application of these new algorithms at large scales will
depend on the continued development of high-resolution (\textless10 m)
global datasets of canopy coverage and height. Such datasets would serve
multiple purposes: they could identify large canopy gaps---many times
larger than the canopy height---where the relationship between leaf
contact area and hydrometeor trajectory angle found in
Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-3} would be expected to break down, and
distinctive sets of energy and mass exchange processes occur (Conway et
al., 2018). Additionally, information on individual tree species would
enable parameterisation of likely species-dependent coefficients,
potentially further refining model accuracy. Beyond canopy
characterization, the reliability of hydrological models and
land-surface schemes depends fundamentally on robust meteorological
forcing data, which is often downscaled to in-situ station observations
(Tang et al., 2023). This underscores the critical importance of
maintaining long-term hydrometeorological stations. Expanding the
monitoring network to areas with limited current coverage---such as
high-elevation and remote regions---would significantly improve the
meteorological data that drives these modelling applications and, by
extension, the quality of informed decision-making.

Future water resource studies and monitoring efforts in forested basins
should directly consider forested areas, which have historically relied
on transfer of SWE measurements from open sites to quantify water
availability in forested basins (Raleigh et al., 2025; Schneider \&
Molotch, 2016). This approach has always been uncertain, and now with
changing forest-snow hydrological regimes associated with climate and
vegetation change are likely to become increasingly unsatisfactory.
These process changes will affect the quantity, phase, and timing of
intercepted snow sublimation, unloading, and drip reaching the ground,
making open-site measurements less representative of total basin water
storage. These findings emphasise the need to expand monitoring networks
to include snow stations under forested canopies that accumulate
significant snowpack, rather than relying solely on open-site locations.

The successful transferability of the new canopy snow interception and
intercepted snow ablation parameterisations across diverse climate zones
and forest types provides evidence of its robustness for application as
a diagnostic tool for investigating impacts of needleleaf canopies on
snow accumulation. When coupled with physically based hydrological
models, this approach offers potential for advancing our understanding
how climate and vegetation changes will influence snow accumulation and
subsequent snowmelt runoff and downstream hydrology. Given that remote
sensing of snow water equivalent in forested environments is expected to
remain limited by the physical interference of forest canopies with
electromagnetic radiation (Rittger et al., 2020), this model-based
diagnostic tool provides researchers with a valuable method for
quantifying water stored as intercepted snow in forest canopies and on
the forest floor, and diagnosing potential future changes. However, it
is important to emphasise that snow interception and ablation in
needleleaf canopies represents only one component of the hydrological
cycle. Once precipitation reaches the ground, numerous other processes
govern how much water ultimately contributes to runoff, including but
not limited to subcanopy interception, evapotranspiration, surface
snowmelt, depressional storage, infiltration, subsurface storage, and
transmission processes. Future research is also needed to help refine
understanding of the aforementioned processes to improve the accuracy of
hydrological models (e.g., Groff \& Pomeroy, 2025). The utility of
hydrological models as diagnostic decision-making tools becomes
increasingly important as climates warm and vegetation distributions
shift. There is an urgent need to understand how these changes will
affect snow accumulation in forests and subsequent runoff. Given the
strong sensitivity of snow interception processes to canopy density and
temperature---which govern the initial interception process and
subsequent melt, unloading, and sublimation of intercepted snow---future
changes in forest structure and climate are expected to significantly
impact snow water storage. Using hydrological models as diagnostic tools
can support decision-makers in developing informed strategies for
diagnosing future water availability and the interactions with climate
and vegetation as environmental conditions continue to evolve.

\chapter{- Appendix for Chapter 2}\label{sec-review-appendix}

\section{Snow Interception Parameterisation
Derivations}\label{snow-interception-parameterisation-derivations}

The original formulation of the Hedstrom \& Pomeroy (1998) snow
interception parameterisation is:

\begin{equation}\phantomsection\label{eq-hp98-int-orig}{
\frac{I}{P}(L) = C_{lca} \frac{L_{max}-L_0-\Delta L}{L_{max}}
}\end{equation}

where \(L_0\) (kg m\textsuperscript{-2}), is the canopy snow load before
snowfall is added to the canopy, \(\Delta L\) (kg
m\textsuperscript{-2}), is the change in canopy snow load due to
snowfall. Equation~\ref{eq-hp98-int-orig} is written in this way in
Hedstrom \& Pomeroy (1998) since they had measurements of \(L_0\) at the
beginning of the storm. However, this equation further simplified here
since:

\[
L = L_0 + \Delta L
\]

and therefore:

\[
\frac{I}{P}(L) = C_{lca}\frac{L_{max}-L}{L_{max}}
\]

The derivation of the Hedstrom \& Pomeroy (1998) analytical snow
interception solution, Equation~\ref{eq-hp98-int-numeric}, from
Equation~\ref{eq-hp98-int-smpl1} is provided by first combining
Equation~\ref{eq-ip} and Equation~\ref{eq-hp98-int-smpl1}:

\begin{equation}\phantomsection\label{eq-hp98-int-smpl}{
\frac{dL}{q_{sf} dt} = C_{lca}\frac{L_{max}-L}{L_{max}}
}\end{equation}

\[\frac{1}{L_{max}-L} dL = \frac{C_{lca}}{L_{max}} q_{sf} dt\]

\[
\int^{L_1}_{L_0}\left[\frac{1}{L_{max}-L}\right] dL =\frac{C_{lca}}{L_{max}} \overline{q_{sf}} \int^{t_1}_{t_0} dt \]
\[-ln\left(\frac{L_{max}-L_1}{L_{max}-L_0}\right)  = \frac{C_{lca}}{L_{max}} \overline{q_{sf}} \Delta t \]
\[\frac{L_{max}-L_1}{L_{max}-L_0} = \text{exp} \left(-\frac{C_{lca}}{L_{max}} \overline{q_{sf}} \Delta t \right)\]
\[1-\frac{L_{max}-L_1}{L_{max}-L_0} = 1- \text{exp} \left(-\frac{C_{lca}}{L_{max}} \overline{q_{sf}} \Delta t \right)\]
\[(L_{max}-L_0)-(L_{max}-L_1) = (L_{max}-L_0)\left(1- \text{exp} \left(-\frac{C_{lca}}{L_{max}} \overline{q_{sf}} \Delta t \right) \right)\]

\begin{equation}\phantomsection\label{eq-interception-integration-steps}{\Delta L = (L_{max}-L_0)\left(1- \text{exp} \left(-\frac{C_{lca}}{L_{max}} \overline{q_{sf}} \Delta t \right) \right)}\end{equation}

here, it is assumed that \(\overline{q_{sf}}\) is the average snowfall
rate over the discrete time interval \(\Delta t\). Since
\(\overline{q_{sf}}\), \(C_{lca}\) and \(L_{max}\) are temporally
constant over the discrete time interval \(\Delta t\) they can be moved
outside the integral. The analytical solution in
Equation~\ref{eq-hp98-int-numeric} is only possible because the snowfall
and throughfall rate are treated in isolation from the other processes
in Equation~\ref{eq-canopy-mass-bal}.

\section{Snow Unloading Parameterisation
Derivations}\label{snow-unloading-parameterisation-derivations}

If the change in canopy snow load due to unloading alone is:

\[
 \frac{d L}{d t} =-q_{unld}(L)
\] then:

Then the steps to get from Equation~\ref{eq-hp98-flu} to
Equation~\ref{eq-hp98-exp-decay} when \(L \ge 0\) are:

\[\frac{1}{L} d L = -\kappa_{unld} \Delta t\]
\[\int^{L(t+\Delta t)}_{L(t)}\frac{1}{L} dL = -\kappa_{unld}\int^{t+\Delta t}_tdt \]
\[ln(L^{t+\Delta t}) - ln(L^t) = -\kappa_{unld} \Delta t \]
\[ ln \left( \frac{L^{t+\Delta t}}{L^t}\right) = -\kappa_{unld}\Delta t\]
\begin{equation}\phantomsection\label{eq-unloading-integration-steps}{L^{t+\Delta t} = L^t \text{exp}^{-\kappa_{unld} \Delta t}}\end{equation}

note since \(\kappa_{unld}\) is temporally constant it can be moved
outside the integral. The analytical solution in
Equation~\ref{eq-hp98-exp-decay} is only possible because unloading is
treated in isolation from the other processes in
Equation~\ref{eq-canopy-mass-bal}.

\pagebreak

\chapter{- Appendix for Chapter 3}\label{sec-appdx-ch3}

\section{Detailed Description of UAV-Lidar
Methodology}\label{detailed-description-of-uav-lidar-methodology}

The REIGL miniVUX-2 laser operates at a near infrared wavelength with a
laser beam footprint of 0.160 m x 0.05 m (at 100 m above ground). The
accuracy and precision of the miniVUX-2 is described by REIGL for a lab
environment of 0.015 m and 0.01 m respectively (at 50 m above ground).
The miniVUX-2 was configured with a laser pulse repetition rate of 200
kHz, field of view of 360°, scan speed of 31.09 revolutions
s\textsuperscript{-1} and an angular step width of 0.0558°, resulting in
an expected an average point cloud density of 107 returns
m\textsuperscript{-2} for each flight path.

Georeferenced point clouds with x, y, and z coordinates for each laser
return were generated following methods outlined by Harder et al. (2020)
and Staines \& Pomeroy (2023) to reconcile survey lidar, IMU and GNSS
data. A ground-based GNSS system was positioned on a permanent monument
during each survey and underwent precise point positioning (PPP)
correction by Natural Resources Canada (2024). Differential GNSS
correction of the UAV trajectory was conducted using the ground-based
PPP GNSS observations and the POSPac UAV software. The UAV-lidar point
clouds were then transformed from a sensor referenced coordinate system
to a georeferenced coordinate system (EPSG:32611 - WGS 84 / UTM zone
11N) using the RIEGL Riprocess Software. A vertical offset of up to 6 cm
between UAV-lidar flight lines was observed in the resulting point
clouds on March 13\textsuperscript{th} and 14\textsuperscript{th}, 2024
and was attributed to IMU position drift. This offset between flight
lines was corrected using the BayesStripAlign software v2.24 (BayesMap
Solutions, 2024), which reduces relative and absolute uncertainties in
the vertical elevation of the point cloud using the ground control
points (GCP) collected across the study site using a differential GNSS
rover.

Quality control, ground classification and calculation of the change in
between two UAV-lidar point clouds was conducted using the LAStools
software package (LAStools, 2024). The ground classification was
conducted using the ``lasground\_new'' function (LAStools, 2024) for
both the pre and post snowfall event point clouds, with a step size set
to 2 m and 8 substeps (ultra\_fine setting). The offset and spike
options were set to remove points that are more than 0.1 m above or
below the initial ground surface estimate surface which
``lasground\_new'' fits to the last returns. This function is based on
an algorithm outlined by Axelsson (2000), describing the process of
making the initial ground surface element.

The change in elevation between the two UAV-lidar surveys was
interpreted as the increase in snow accumulation, \(\Delta HS\) over the
snowfall event. This change was calculated using a point-to-grid
subtraction method, using the ``lasheight'' function from the LAStools
(2024) software, as in Deems et al. (2013) and Staines \& Pomeroy
(2023). The pre snowfall event point cloud from ``lasground\_new'' by
``lasheight'' to construct a ``ground'' TIN. Subsequently, the height of
each post snowfall event point above the ground TIN, resulting in a
point cloud representing \(\Delta HS\). This point cloud was then
converted into a raster of \(\Delta HS\) with a grid cell resolution of
5 x 5 cm using the ``las2dem'' function. Further quality control and
resampling of the 5 cm raster of \(\Delta HS\) was conducted using the
`Terra' R package (Hijmans, 2024). Areas that were disturbed over the
snowfall event during the in-situ snow survey and values that exceeded
the .999th quantile were removed. To help remove any remaining noise a
0.25 m \(\Delta HS\) raster was generated by computing the median of the
5 cm \(\Delta HS\) values within each 0.25 m grid cell.

A comparison of UAV-liar and in-situ snow survey measurements over the
March 13--14th snowfall event and associated error metrics are shown in
Figure~\ref{fig-lidar-vs-fsd-sd}.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/03-snow-int-paper/figs/figureS1.png}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-lidar-vs-fsd-sd}UAV-liar and in-situ snow survey
measurements over the March 13--14th snowfall event and associated error
metrics.}

\end{figure}%

\section{Linear Regression Models Through the
Origin}\label{linear-regression-models-through-the-origin}

Kozak \& Kozak (1995) noted, the default R\textsuperscript{2} value
provided for least squares models forced through the origin by many
statistical packages can be misleading. Therefore, these
R\textsuperscript{2} values were adjusted using Equation 10 in Kozak \&
Kozak (1995) and two statistical tests as described by Kozak \& Kozak
(1995) were used to verify whether a no-intercept model (forced through
the origin) was appropriate for this data compared to a with-intercept
model. The first test evaluated if the intercept of the with-intercept
was significantly different from zero using p-value provided by the
`summary' function from the `stats' package in R (R Core Team, 2024).
The second test examined if there was a significant difference between
the no-intercept and with-intercept models by testing if the residual
sum of squares was different between the no-intercept and full model,
assessed via Equation 15 in Kozak \& Kozak (1995). If the first test
indicated a significant difference, and the second did not, the
no-intercept model could be deemed statistically justified (Kozak \&
Kozak, 1995).

\section{Unloading Relationships}\label{unloading-relationships}

Canopy snow unloading measured by the subcanopy weighed snow buckets
over all observed unloading events shows some of the melt-driven and
dry-snow unloading process emergence. Some diverging process emergence
is also illustrated by increased unloading during low wind periods
driven by increased canopy snowmelt rates, increased unloading resulting
from negative sublimation rates due to the association with melt, and
increased unloading with sublimation up until 0.3 mm/hr possibly due to
the association with wind speed.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{chapters/04-ablation-paper/figs/supplement/figureS3.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-q-unld-all-bins}Scatter plots showing the mean
unloading rate (mm hr\textsuperscript{-1}) for differing bins of air
temperature (°C), ice-bulb temperature depression (°C), shear stress (N
m\textsuperscript{-2}), canopy snowmelt (mm hr\textsuperscript{-1}),
canopy snow sublimation (mm hr\textsuperscript{-1}), and wind speed (m
s\textsuperscript{-1}). Note: unloading was measured by the subcanopy
weighed snow buckets, air temperature and wind speed were measured at FT
station, canopy snowmelt and sublimation were calculated using CRHM. The
canopy snow load corresponds to the midpoint of each bin (0--2, 2--6,
6--20 kg m\textsuperscript{-2}).}

\end{figure}%

\chapter{- Appendix for Chapter 5}\label{sec-appdx-ch5}

\section{Description of New Modules added to the Cold Regions
Hydrological Modelling Platform
(CRHM)}\label{description-of-new-modules-added-to-the-cold-regions-hydrological-modelling-platform-crhm}

The following describes the new modules added to the CRHM software as
part of this research. The new modules are actively developed in the
CRHM repository
\href{https://github.com/acebulsk/crhmcode/blob/alex-ceb-thesis-changes}{\color{blue}{here}}
and specific changes are documented in pull request
\href{https://github.com/srlabUsask/crhmcode/pull/471}{\color{blue}{\#471}}.
The version of CRHM used in this manuscript has been permanently
archived
\href{https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.17981431}{\color{blue}{here}}. The
CRHM modules listed below are located at the path and point to the
version being actively being developed:

\href{https://github.com/acebulsk/crhmcode/blob/alex-ceb-thesis-changes/crhmcode/src/modules}{\color{blue}{https://github.com/acebulsk/crhmcode/blob/alex-ceb-thesis-changes/crhmcode/src/modules}}

\subsection{Initial Snow Interception}\label{initial-snow-interception}

\textbf{Description:}

A new module called \texttt{CanopyVectorBased} was added to CRHM to
compute initial snow interception in the canopy as described in
Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-3}. This module differs from the existing CRHM
\texttt{CanopyClearingGap} module based on Ellis et al. (2010) which
calculated initial interception as a function of antecedent canopy snow
load and canopy density. The new module represents an improvement by
calculating initial interception as a function of snowfall and canopy
density (function of canopy coverage and hydrometeor trajectory angle)
based on observations presented in Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-3}. The
association of interception with canopy snow load is now handled only
once by the canopy snow unloading routine in the canopy snow ablation
module.

\textbf{Modules:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \href{https://github.com/acebulsk/crhmcode/blob/alex-ceb-thesis-changes/crhmcode/src/modules/ClassCRHMCanopyVectorBased.cpp}{\color{blue}{ClassCRHMCanopyVectorBased.cpp}}
\item
  \href{https://github.com/acebulsk/crhmcode/blob/alex-ceb-thesis-changes/crhmcode/src/modules/ClassCRHMCanopyVectorBased.h}{\color{blue}{ClassCRHMCanopyVectorBased.h}}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Canopy Snow Energy and Mass
Balance}\label{canopy-snow-energy-and-mass-balance}

\textbf{Description:}

A new module called \texttt{CanopySnowBalance} simulates the
physically-based energy and mass balance of snow stored in forest
canopies following developments from Chapter~\ref{sec-chapter-4}. It is
designed to be paired with the \texttt{CanopyVectorBased} module and
together with \texttt{CanopySnowBalance} replaces the
\texttt{CanopyClearingGap} module in CRHM. The
\texttt{CanopySnowBalance} represents both dry- and melt-induced canopy
snow unloading coupled with energy balance-based melt and sublimation
calculations.

\textbf{Modules:}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \href{https://github.com/acebulsk/crhmcode/blob/alex-ceb-thesis-changes/crhmcode/src/modules/ClassCanopySnowBalanceCRHM.cpp}{\color{blue}{ClassCanopySnowBalanceCRHM.cpp}}
\item
  \href{https://github.com/acebulsk/crhmcode/blob/alex-ceb-thesis-changes/crhmcode/src/modules/ClassCanopySnowBalanceCRHM.h}{\color{blue}{ClassCanopySnowBalanceCRHM.h}}
\item
  \href{https://github.com/acebulsk/crhmcode/blob/alex-ceb-thesis-changes/crhmcode/src/modules/ClassCanopySnowBalanceBase.cpp}{\color{blue}{ClassCanopySnowBalanceBase.cpp}}
\item
  \href{https://github.com/acebulsk/crhmcode/blob/alex-ceb-thesis-changes/crhmcode/src/modules/ClassCanopySnowBalanceBase.h}{\color{blue}{ClassCanopySnowBalanceBase.h}}
\end{itemize}

\section{Model Evaluation}\label{model-evaluation-2}

Simulated canopy snow load at Marmot Creek and subcanopy SWE at the four
forest plots by the two models (E10 and CP25) was evaluated using
observations. The performance of the two models was evaluated based on
the differences in simulated (\(S_i\)) and observed (\(O_i\)) values of
SWE using mean bias (MB), root mean squared error (RMSE), Nash-Sutcliffe
efficiency (NSE, Nash \& Sutcliffe, 1970), and Kling-Gupta Efficiency
(KGE, Gupta et al., 2009; Clark et al., 2021) as:

\begin{equation}\phantomsection\label{eq-mb}{
\text{MB} = \frac{1}{n} \sum^n_{i=1}(S_i-O_i)
}\end{equation}

\begin{equation}\phantomsection\label{eq-rmse}{
\text{RMSE} = \sqrt{\frac{\sum^n_{i=1}(S_i-O_i)^2}{n}}
}\end{equation}

\begin{equation}\phantomsection\label{eq-nse}{
\text{NSE} = 1 - \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} (S_i - O_i)^2}{\sum_{i=1}^{n} (O_i - \bar{O})^2}
}\end{equation}

\begin{equation}\phantomsection\label{eq-kge}{
\text{KGE} = 1 - \sqrt{\left(\frac{\bar{S}}{\bar{O}}-1\right)^2 + (\alpha - 1)^2 + (\rho_p - 1)^2}
}\end{equation}

where \(\bar{O}\) and \(\bar{S}\) are the means of the observed and
simulated values, respectively, \(n\) is the number of observations,
\(\alpha\) is the ratio of simulated to observed standard deviation, and
\(\rho_p\) is the Pearson correlation the simulated and observed values.
